Technology atlas

Historical changes

Existing paired model-reviewed claims, bound to the exact workflow versions. Similarity and component adoption do not imply whole-method descent or interchangeability.

Quartz-Seq → Quartz-Seq2 · method_development

Retained / shared arrangement

Retained arrangement: the original Sasagawa et al. Quartz-Seq assay (PMID 23594475; DOI 10.1186/gb-2013-14-4-r31) is the explicitly named starting point of Sasagawa et al. Quartz-Seq2 (PMID 29523163). The affected portion is oligo(dT)-primed first-strand synthesis followed by TdT addition of a DNA poly(A) tail, poly(dT)-tagging-primer second-strand synthesis installing PCR interfaces, and PCR-based WTA. The earlier arrangement is independently described in the original paper, not inferred only from the later account. The selected right group also contains revised handling before WTA and the combined library block; inheritance is confined to the retained arrangement, not every constituent of those blocks.

What changed

1. Recording and cell attribution (left add_rt_primer/reverse_transcribe_digest; right lysis_rt, with pool_purify downstream): the original RT primer supplies oligo-dT24, a T7 promoter and PCR target, and each single-cell WTA remains a separate tube preparation. Quartz-Seq2 incorporates a 14/15-nt cell barcode and an 8-nt UMI, together with oligo-dT24 and the PCR interface, into first-strand cDNA before pooling. Cell identity and molecule tags are now carried by DNA; the UMI is not supplied by TdT tailing. The right main text describes SeqLv-designed barcode sets and correction of up to two barcode errors, rather than a comparable error-correcting cell-barcode codebook on the left (left Fig. 1/Table 1 and b00015; right b00007/b00011/b00074). 2. Residual-primer handling (left reverse_transcribe_digest; right lysis_rt): original Quartz-Seq digests unused primer with exonuclease I, incompletely, before tailing; Quartz-Seq2 omits that step with its selected 73/74-mer RT primer design. The right paper's relatively short design is relative to problematic longer designs tested in that system; it is not evidence that the original Quartz-Seq primer was longer. This changes the preparation entering tailing and the source of potential primer-derived byproducts (left b00016/b00021; right b00011). 3. RT conditions (same records): original SuperScript III RT uses PCR-buffer/DTT conditions and 35°C for 5 min then 45°C for 20 min. Quartz-Seq2 uses a Thermopol-based RT premix, 35°C for 5 min then 50°C for 50 min, and mainly the RT25 low-enzyme condition, including lower SuperScript III and RNasin concentrations relative to its RT100 condition. Buffer, temperature/time and enzyme-condition changes are recorded together; no isolated causal gain is assigned to any one of them (left b00077; right b00011/b00018/b00078). 4. Organization before WTA (left single-cell WTA group; right pool_purify between lysis_rt and tail): the left completes WTA in one PCR tube per cell without intermediate purification. The right spin-collects cell-barcoded RT products from plate wells, pools and column-purifies/concentrates first-strand cDNA before shared tailing and amplification; v3.2 permits pooling 1536 cells. Incorporated cell barcodes, rather than continued separate WTA tubes, preserve cell assignment after mixing (left b00015/b00077; right b00007/b00011/b00078-b00079). 5. Poly(A) tagging (left tail_and_add_tagging_primer + second_strand; right tail + second_strand): the original uses a PCR-buffer-based tailing reaction restricted to 50 s and second-strand synthesis at 40°C then 68°C without the described Increment ramp. The selected Quartz-Seq2 combination uses T55 tailing buffer and Increment tagging/second-strand conditions; its Methods tail for 75 s and heat from 40°C to 68°C at 0.2°C/s before extension. Retained TdT/poly(dT) chemistry acts on different preparations: left unpurified individual cDNA with possible residual-primer substrates, right pooled purified barcode/UMI-cDNA. Keep T55 plus Increment together for the approximately 3.6-fold yield result (left b00023/b00077; right b00013-b00015/b00078). 6. Amplification (left add_suppression_primer + suppression_pcr; amplification component of right amplify_library): the original explicitly adds the Table 1 amino-modified suppression primer and uses 21 WTA PCR cycles, suppressing short complementary-ended byproducts. Quartz-Seq2 adds gM primer and describes 11 WTA cycles for v3.1 or nine for v3.2 pooled samples. This is not an isolated cycle-count intervention: input pooling and preceding chemistry also differ. The main text does not establish transfer of an LNA blocker or independently establish that right-hand gM PCR has identical short-template suppression behavior (left b00016/b00022/b00077-b00078; right b00078-b00079). 7. Library and interpretation (left suppression_pcr endpoint; right amplify_library): the left graph ends at amplified cDNA; the original paper subsequently uses Covaris fragmentation and adaptor ligation for paired-end sequencing, with fragment-based expression reported as FPKM. The right combined block additionally fragments amplified cDNA, ligates a truncated Y adaptor carrying P7 and a pool barcode, and enriches cell-barcode/UMI-bearing fragments. Its library is read as CB/UMI in Read1, PB in Index1 and transcript sequence in Read2, supporting per-cell, per-gene UMI counts from successfully captured molecules. The pool barcode distinguishes reused cell-barcode sets. This library structure is not claimed wholly inherited from the original: the later paper separately credits Drop-seq for sequence-library/data-processing design (left b00017/b00029/b00082; right b00007/b00094).

Reported consequence and limits

Observed results and author interpretation are distinct. The original reports removal of byproduct synthesis using the combined exonuclease-I/restricted-tailing/suppression-PCR arrangement. The later paper reports approximately 3.6-fold amplified-cDNA yield for T55 plus Increment, a yield assay on first-strand cDNA derived from 1 ng total RNA (C3), not a 3.6-fold read-to-UMI result. With its adopted combined improvements, Quartz-Seq2 yielded 44,100 ± 7521 UMIs and 7442 ± 484 genes versus 18,407 ± 4040 UMIs and 5728 ± 604 genes under Quartz-Seq-like conditions, using 10 pg diluted mouse total RNA per well at approximately 0.19 M initial reads/well; UMI conversion was about 22.88% versus 9.55%. This is a modified, barcoded control described in the later Methods, not the unchanged, non-UMI original assay. The authors' explanation that improved poly(A) tagging contributes to better UMI conversion is supported as their interpretation of combined-workflow results, not a separately isolated benefit of TdT, the buffer, or the temperature ramp. Reagent-preparation cost comparisons are scoped in C5. No safe substitution, complete inheritance or equal performance follows.

Reviewer reasoning

The shared method_development definition is met: a documented starting assay with a retained and revised arrangement. Right b00008 explicitly states that Quartz-Seq2 was developed based on Quartz-Seq [15]; right b00163 and the parsed CR15 bibliography identify the cited implementation as Sasagawa et al., PMID 23594475, DOI 10.1186/gb-2013-14-4-r31. This is substantive assay attribution, unlike the same reference being used elsewhere for cell culture or general background. Independently, left b00015-b00017, Fig. 1 and Methods b00077-b00078 demonstrate the earlier arrangement: an oligo(dT)/T7/M primer copies polyadenylated RNA, exonuclease I removes most unused primer, TdT tails first-strand cDNA and surviving primers, a poly(dT) tagging primer generates second-strand DNA with WTA interfaces, and suppression-primer PCR yields amplified cDNA. These stages remain in an individual-cell tube without intervening purification; residual-primer DNA is co-present, not a separately routed fraction. This reconstructs the evidenced starting assay rather than inferring it from the later paper. The retained core in right b00007/b00011/b00013/b00078 is oligo(dT) RT followed by DNA poly(A) tailing, tagging-primer second-strand synthesis and PCR. The necessary coordinated revisions are explicitly covered: cell barcode/UMI recording in first-strand DNA; omission of exonuclease I under the selected 73/74-mer primer design; altered RT buffer, temperature/time and RT25 enzyme conditions; collection, pooling and purification before WTA; T55 plus Increment and the changed tailing/second-strand program; pooled WTA amplification conditions; and the combined barcode-preserving library construction and read interface. Original b00077 and right b00078 independently anchor the changed conditions. Original cell attribution uses separate preparations, whereas right molecular tags preserve attribution after physical wells are pooled; tailed and second-strand DNA then supplies PCR interfaces for the next amplification. Right Fig. 1 and b00084-b00085 connect amplified DNA through fragmentation, truncated-Y adaptor ligation and selective enrichment to the CB/UMI/PB-bearing sequencing library. Those internal actions remain paper annotations to the existing amplify_library block. The left graph ends at amplified cDNA, and left b00017/b00082-b00083 place its actual library construction downstream, so the endpoint difference is not an assay-wide absence of library preparation. Right b00094 separately attributes library/data-processing design to Drop-seq; C1 correctly avoids complete workflow inheritance and does not duplicate this assertion as component_adoption. Group-tool inspection confirms the selected six left operations span rna to amplified_cdna, and the selected five right operations span wells to library, with sort immediately upstream on the right. Every selected group is connected and has no routed side branch. The quantified consequences are correctly scoped: original byproduct suppression is attributed to its combined intervention; the 3.6-fold observation is amplified-cDNA yield under joint T55/Increment conditions, and the 10-pg, approximately 0.19-M-read comparison is against the later barcoded Quartz-Seq-like control, not unchanged original Quartz-Seq. Right b00054 presents improved tagging as the authors’ explanation for better UMI conversion. The claim does not turn that explanation into separately proven benefits of each change, equal capture, safe substitution, or priority. Its seven-part before/after account covers the necessary revisions at its stated RT-to-WTA/library scope.

Drop-seq → Quartz-Seq2 · component_adoption

Retained / shared arrangement

Adoption of Drop-seq's sequence-library information arrangement: paired library molecules/read roles connect a cell barcode and UMI in Read1 to transcript-derived cDNA in Read2, supporting per-cell, per-gene digital expression after interpretation. This contribution spans the left library/sequence group and is embodied in the right amplify_library output's prospective read interface. Only that arrangement is inherited.

What changed

Retained arrangement: Read1 identifies cell/molecule and the paired Read2 identifies transcript sequence on a DNA reporter. 1. LEFT demonstrates this in bead-derived 3′ expression libraries and actual paired sequencing (Fig. 2C–D). RIGHT explicitly says its library structure and data processing were designed based on Drop-seq and implements the same paired read roles after well RT and poly(A)-tagging amplification. The attribution identifies Macosko et al. as reference [6] (PMID 26000488); this is the registered left implementation. 2. LEFT uses 12-base bead-cell barcodes; RIGHT uses designed 14/15-base cell barcodes and 8-base UMIs, with 22/23-cycle Read1 specifications (b00088). RIGHT also reads its pool barcode at Index1 to distinguish combined barcode sets; this does not change the function of the cell barcode or UMI. 3. LEFT group includes actual acquisition of reads. RIGHT graph stops at a library ready for sequencing: its read assignments are material-interface annotations, not a nonexistent right sequencing operation. 4. RIGHT retains this information arrangement while its main construction uses Covaris/adapter ligation and its upstream substrate is well-derived cDNA. Adoption of the read architecture does not establish adoption of those upstream processes.

Reported consequence and limits

The inherited arrangement enables linkage of cell/molecule identifiers with transcript sequence and compatible interpretation in the demonstrated workflows. No measured gain is assigned specifically to adopting it. The pair's reported UMI-conversion and gene-detection advantages are whole-workflow comparisons (c7), not evidence that this adoption improved performance.

Reviewer reasoning

Accepted as written under the shared definition of component_adoption: a named adopted contribution, its earlier demonstration, and its destination in the later workflow are established. The contribution is the paired library/read information arrangement, not the whole assay and not the omitted computational pipeline. Starting with the earlier implementation, Drop-seq Fig. 2C independently demonstrates a library whose Read1 supplies cell barcode/UMI and paired Read2 supplies transcript-derived cDNA sequence; Fig. 2D and b00017 explain how those associations support per-cell/per-gene digital expression. Later Quartz-Seq2 Methods b00094 explicitly attributes the sequence-library structure to Drop-seq [6]; the registered bibliography identifies [6] as Macosko et al., PMID 26000488, the actual left paper. Later Fig. 1 and b00088 show actual use: its well-derived, poly(A)-tagged cDNA library retains Read1 CB/UMI and Read2 transcript roles, adapts to 14/15-base cell barcodes with 22/23-cycle Read1, and adds PB at Index1. The resulting DNA library therefore presents the retained identity/transcript linkage to subsequent sequencing and expression interpretation. The left make_library + sequence group ends in reads, whereas the destination right amplify_library ends in material library DNA; group inspection verifies that asymmetry, and the claim explicitly represents the later read roles as a prospective material-interface annotation. No right sequencing operation is invented. This is an attributable library-design contribution retained across different upstream preparation chemistry. It is distinct from c6's adopted Nextera procedure; one could retain these read roles using the main Covaris construction. Neither the structural similarity nor the direct performance comparison establishes assay development from Drop-seq. The later source instead identifies original Quartz-Seq [15], PMID 23594475, as its assay basis; that original paper is not available for an independently verified external development assertion.

Drop-seq → Quartz-Seq2 · component_adoption

Retained / shared arrangement

Separately scoped procedural adoption: preparing a barcode-retaining sequencing library from amplified cDNA with Nextera XT and custom amplification primers. The earlier Drop-seq implementation demonstrates this procedure; the later Quartz-Seq2 paper explicitly reports an additional Tn5/Nextera library-preparation variant modified from reference [6]. This claim covers only that paper-reported variant inside the broad library block, not the drawn main Covaris/ligation route or either entire Operation.

What changed

Retained arrangement: amplified transcript-derived cDNA is processed with Nextera XT and custom PCR primers to retain the informative barcode-linked library end. 1. LEFT make_library uses Nextera XT and custom primers for specific 3′-end amplification (b00056). RIGHT b00086 says it also prepared libraries with Tn5 transposase according to a modified procedure from [6], verified as Macosko's Drop-seq paper. 2. RIGHT specifies 0.75 ng amplified cDNA and PCR with P5-gMac hybrid primer plus a Nextera XT primer carrying P7. The accessible left main text does not establish an input-mass comparison or exact identities of all custom primer constructs; its Table S6/extended procedure is not separately registered. Thus the supported adaptation is to Quartz-Seq2-derived cDNA with the stated later primer arrangement, not a claim that every primer or reagent is identical. 3. RIGHT Fig. 1 and the existing graph depict the main Covaris fragmentation/truncated-Y-adapter route. Its additional Nextera use is a bounded paper annotation within amplify_library, with no alternate Operation ID or branch invented.

Reported consequence and limits

Actual use of the adapted library procedure is reported in Quartz-Seq2. No variant-specific yield, UMI-conversion, gene-count or equal-performance result is established here. In particular, the main workflow's reported performance cannot automatically be assigned to this additional variant or generalized as safe substitution between the two library-construction routes.

Reviewer reasoning

Accepted as written under the shared definition of component_adoption: an identifiable experimental procedure is explicitly attributed and used at a specified destination, and the cited earlier paper independently demonstrates it. In the earlier Drop-seq implementation, b00056 processes amplified cDNA with Nextera XT and custom primers selecting the informative 3′ ends; Fig. 2C establishes the ensuing barcode/UMI-to-transcript read linkage. The later Quartz-Seq2 b00086 explicitly says it also prepared library DNA using a modified Tn5 procedure from [6], which the bibliography independently identifies as the same Macosko Drop-seq implementation. The retained contribution is Nextera XT processing of amplified transcript-derived cDNA plus custom library amplification. At the later destination, upstream well RT and TdT/poly(dT) amplification supply Quartz-Seq2-derived cDNA; b00086 specifies 0.75 ng input and amplification with P5-gMac hybrid and a Nextera XT P7-bearing primer, yielding sequencing-library DNA for the paired information arrangement documented in the later paper. The exact earlier input mass and full primer identities are unavailable in the registered main text, and the claim correctly avoids claiming a quantified mass change or identical constructs. The group tool shows pooled_beads -> library for left make_library and amplifiable_cdna -> library for right amplify_library, with no alternate branch in either graph. The claim confines this adoption to a paper-reported alternative inside the broad right library block; it does not say the depicted main Covaris/ligation implementation inherited Nextera. Actual use, not a presumed performance gain, supports adoption. This procedure is distinct from c5's read-layout design and is not a second historical label for that same assertion.

Nuc-Seq → snRNA-Seq · method_development

Retained / shared arrangement

Scoped procedural development from the Krishnaswami et al. nuclear RNA-seq preparation implementation (PMID 26890679) into the Lacar et al. activated-neuron preparation (PMID 27090946). The retained connected strategy is cold Dounce/detergent release of nuclei, nuclear labelling and fluorescence selection of individual nuclei that supply separately prepared nuclear transcriptomes. The affected portion is nuclei preparation and selection, represented by left isolate+sort and right isolate_nuclei+sort_nuclei; this assertion does not extend to the entire frozen-tissue-to-library workflow.

What changed

Retained arrangement: the cited left implementation independently describes intact-nucleus release with Triton X-100/Dounce, Hoechst identification and optional neuronal immunostaining followed by singlet FACS into lysis buffer. Right explicitly states that it follows a modified published nuclei-isolation procedure, citing references 13 and 32; reference 32 is the selected left paper. 1. Cleanup revision within isolate → isolate_nuclei: right states that gradient centrifugation was omitted. Checking the cited left implementation limits this revision: its gradient cleanup is already optional and its default is filtered homogenate directly to FACS. Thus right commits to a no-gradient implementation of the documented preparation arrangement; it has not demonstrated invention of the no-gradient option, nor does it eliminate all centrifugation. 2. Target selection revision within sort → sort_nuclei: the left procedure offers NeuN neuronal enrichment or general DNA-stained nuclei. Right incorporates the specified NEUN/PROX1/FOS immunostaining and gating arrangement to select DGCs and relate their nuclear transcriptomes to FOS protein status. This changes which nuclei enter downstream synthesis and what their profiles are conditioned on. 'An antibody staining protocol was included' does not mean that the left implementation lacked immunostaining; the meaningful change is the marker combination and activation-oriented selection. 3. Application context at these group boundaries: the drawn left input is frozen postmortem tissue, but the cited protocol also supports fresh tissue; the right uses freshly dissected mouse brain after controlled experiences. Freezing is outside this historical group and is not asserted to have been adopted. Downstream Smart-seq2/Nextera use is documented separately by each paper and is not thereby inherited through this preparation claim.

Reported consequence and limits

The right combined preparation and analysis reports Arc detection in 81% of NE FOS+ versus 2% of NE FOS− nuclei (P<1.9e−11), showing that sorted protein-defined populations can reveal experience-associated nuclear RNA differences in this setting. This is a within-right biological comparison, not a demonstration that the revisions outperform the cited left preparation. The papers do not isolate a recovery or sensitivity gain from either omission of gradient cleanup or the revised marker panel.

Reviewer reasoning

The method_development definition is met at the stated nuclei-preparation/selection scope: a documented starting nuclear RNA-seq assay arrangement is retained and revised, rather than an isolated reagent merely being shared. The named implementations are Krishnaswami et al., PMID 26890679, and Lacar et al., PMID 27090946. Right Results b00024 says that nuclei were isolated following a modified published procedure, with citations 13 and 32; the actual reference-32 entry (b00121; parsed bibliography b32) identifies the selected Krishnaswami implementation. This is procedural attribution, not an introductory citation or inference from dates. Right Methods b00056 separately cites Grindberg for dissociation; that does not remove the explicit additional attribution to Krishnaswami in Results. Only this one historical label is assigned to the assertion. The earlier arrangement is independently supported by the left original: cold Dounce/Triton release preserves nuclei and includes Hoechst identification (b00013, Procedure b00213), optional neuronal immunostaining and singlet FACS produce individual nuclei in lysis buffer (b00016, b00218-b00238), and these nuclei supply RNA for cDNA synthesis (b00251). Its gradient is already optional with filtered homogenate normally going directly to FACS (b00008); Procedure b00216 specifically recommends that direct route for frozen human tissue. Starting from that arrangement, right b00024 commits to omitting the gradient, while b00056 documents actual cold Dounce/detergent preparation, washing, resuspension and filtering. Thus the claim accurately describes implementation of a no-gradient option, without inventing a new no-gradient capability or removal of every centrifugation. The coordinated selection revision is also established on both sides. Earlier optional NeuN/general-nuclei selection becomes the documented DGC/activation-oriented PROX1, NEUN and FOS staining/gating arrangement (right b00024-b00026, b00060). The right paper's statement that antibody staining was included cannot mean that the cited left method lacked immunostaining; C2 explicitly avoids that misreading. The resulting material is separately deposited, protein-phenotyped nuclear preparations, not purified protein or molecularly barcoded RNA. Right b00031-b00032 connects NE PROX1/FOS sorting to plate Smart-seq2 transcriptome amplification, and b00034 supplies the downstream Arc RNA detection comparison between FOS-selected populations. Those passages support the necessary preparation -> selected nucleus -> transcript profile connection. They do not prove a causal gain from either procedural revision. The Arc 81% versus 2% figures and P value describe detection in the right NE FOS groups; they do not compare the two assays. Freezing, cDNA primer design/cycling, and library handling are excluded from the claimed inheritance, as written. The left reverse citation to Lacar in b00054/R28 supports Fos/experience observations and is listed as in press; it does not attribute adoption of a preparation procedure in the opposite direction. Exact version exchange and priority remain unknown. These limits do not defeat the directly attributed, independently checked preparation arrangement or turn it into an entire-workflow ancestry claim.

The detergent can also permeabilize and lyse the nuclear envelope, but only under harsh conditions for an extended period of time22. Sufficient Triton X-100 is included in the homogenization step to facilitate the release of nuclei, allowing them to remain intact, and to permit optimal antibody18 staining and isolation by FACS without forming aggregates. Hoechst stain is added to the homogenization lysis buffer to identify nuclei during FACS."

Second, we now use Smart-seq2 for cDNA synthesis3 (instead of the method by Tang et al.5), which is reported to improve synthesis of full-length cDNA via a template switching mechanism for synthesis of the second-strand cDNA4."

Alternatively, nuclei from all cell types can be sorted by using nuclear staining with either Hoechst or propidium iodide (PI) (Fig. 3d,f). Dounce homogenization21 with a nonionic surfactant, Triton X-100, is used to lyse the cell membrane and release nuclei. The detergent can also permeabilize and lyse the nuclear envelope, but only under harsh conditions for an extended period of time22. Sufficient Triton X-100 is included in the homogenization step to facilitate the release of nuclei, allowing them to remain intact, and to permit optimal antibody18 staining and isolation by FACS without forming aggregates. Hoechst stain is added to the homogenization lysis buffer to identify nuclei during FACS."

Drop-seq → DroNc-seq · method_development

Retained / shared arrangement

Macosko et al.'s Drop-seq implementation is the documented starting assay for Habib et al.'s DroNc-seq droplet-to-readout arrangement. Retained: separate biological-particle and barcoded-bead streams are co-encapsulated; released RNA is captured on the companion bead's oligo(dT) primers; droplets are broken before bulk RT; bead-derived barcode/UMI information passes into amplified cDNA, a Nextera-derived DNA library, and paired sequencing reads. The biological input is a cell on the left and an isolated nucleus on the right. This is development of that connected arrangement, not inheritance of every preparation, parameter or analysis.

What changed

Retained arrangement: biological particle plus barcoded oligo(dT) bead in a droplet, intradroplet RNA capture, bead recovery, then bulk cDNA/library production and paired readout (left load through sequence; right encapsulate through sequence). 1. Biological input and measurement unit: left load receives an intact single-cell suspension, and left lyse_capture samples cellular polyadenylated mRNAs (Macosko b00009, b00011, b00015). Right encapsulate receives isolated nuclei; its upstream, unmatched illumina:dronc_seq:operation:isolate_nuclei uses EZ PREP and a PBS/BSA/RNase-inhibitor suspension rather than treating intact cells as the droplet input (Habib b00027, b00030-b00032). Right lyse_capture therefore samples the nuclear RNA compartment. Habib b00006 identifies lower RNA amount in nuclei as the motivation for adaptation. Frozen-brain applications annotate the drawn preparation boundary without adding a branch. Nucleus isolation is an added upstream preparation here, not an inherited Drop-seq nucleus-isolation operation. 2. Compartment scale and bead interface, considered together: left load generates approximately 1 nL droplets with beads in lysis buffer and cells in a separate aqueous stream (Macosko b00015, b00056). Right encapsulate uses approximately 75 micrometer, 200 pL droplets, 70 micrometer-wide channels and 75 micrometer channel depth; the later paper identifies its Drop-seq device comparator as producing 125 micrometer drops. Right retains beads prepared as Ref 7 but selects beads below 40 micrometers, roughly 55% of its purchased pool, because large beads can clog the narrower channels (Habib b00019, b00031-b00032). The original main text establishes bead synthesis/loading but supplies no directly comparable bead size-selection distribution. The supported adaptation is right's stated selection at its bead-input boundary; no original bead-lot size distribution or absence of selection is invented. The smaller compartment concentrates the limited nuclear RNA before capture while retaining the oligo(dT) selector. 3. Loading and flow were coordinated with the device: left b00015 describes dilute cell and bead occupancy, and b00022 tests 12.5-100 cells per microliter. Right b00030-b00033 uses approximately 300 nuclei and 350 beads per microliter, aqueous streams at 1.5 ml/hr each and oil at 16 ml/hr, with nucleus Poisson loading about 0.07. These right settings govern multiplets and bead flow in encapsulate. Raw particle concentration is not equivalent occupancy when droplet size changes. Right's optimization used species mixing and ease of bead flow together; no isolated effect of one setting is inferred. 4. Recovery and bulk-processing placement: left recover_pool breaks the emulsion as soon as droplet generation is complete and sends washed mRNA-bound beads into RT, then aliquots beads for PCR and pools PCR products (Macosko b00056). Right recover_beads follows approximately 22-minute collections held at room temperature for up to 45 minutes before breaking and RT (Habib b00036). Right make_cdna explicitly pools cDNA-bearing STAMPs from collections of a given sample after RT, then amplifies bead aliquots and prepares a Nextera library (b00038-b00040). Both retain break-before-RT ordering. Later STAMP pooling is inside broad make_cdna, distinct from earlier RNA-bead recovery, without an invented internal operation. Left's described PCR-product pooling does not establish that other STAMP pooling was forbidden. Sample-dependent PCR and sequencing settings are qualified in C4 and are not evidence of an independently redesigned chemistry.

Reported consequence and limits

Habib b00032 states that smaller drops give more than fivefold higher mRNA concentration than its 125 micrometer Drop-seq comparator. This is the authors' concentration explanation, not a separately established fivefold gain in molecule recovery, gene detection or accuracy. Habib b00033 reports that its selected bead/nucleus loading combination performed best among tested settings for low species-mixed doublets and fewer clogs, without assigning individual causal effects. Habib b00034 reports similar results for 3T3 nuclei in the 125 and 75 micrometer devices; no universal sensitivity gain from smaller droplets is established. Whole-workflow cell-versus-nucleus measurements are in C5. The nucleus-isolation time/degradation explanation in Habib b00028 compares its EZ PREP route with a sucrose-gradient sNuc-Seq preparation, not with a nucleus-isolation step in Drop-seq.

Reviewer reasoning

Accept as written. This satisfies method_development's documented starting-assay definition: Habib b00006 says that DroNc-seq modifies Drop-seq, and its Ref 7 is the supplied Macosko implementation (title, DOI and PMID checked in the registered bibliography and paper). Crucially, the later procedural contexts attribute bead preparation, loading, breaking/RT, amplification and Nextera processing to that reference (b00031-b00040); the background citation and dates are not the historical evidence. Independently, Macosko b00009-b00017, b00056 and Figure 2 demonstrate the starting arrangement: cell suspension plus barcoded oligo(dT) beads/lysis buffer becomes a droplet emulsion; lysis releases cellular poly(A) RNA onto bead primers; breaking and washing yield RNA-bearing beads for bulk RT; bead-primed cDNA acquires barcode/UMI linkage, is amplified and converted to a 3-prime DNA library for paired barcode/transcript readout. Habib retains these connected material and information interfaces while changing the biological particle to an isolated nucleus. Its EZ PREP/NSB isolation supplies that new boundary input, without an alleged Drop-seq nucleus-isolation ancestor. For lower nuclear RNA, its stated coordinated implementation uses approximately 75-micrometer/200-pL droplets, narrower channels, selection of beads below 40 micrometers, and revised particle concentrations and flow (b00019, b00027, b00030-b00033). Macosko independently supplies the approximately 1-nL baseline; the 125-micrometer comparator diameter comes from Habib. The record does not invent an original bead-size distribution or claim that original bead selection was absent. Nuclear RNA-bearing beads still enter bulk RT after breaking; Habib then explicitly pools cDNA-bearing STAMPs across collections after RT, amplifies bead aliquots, and prepares the Nextera library before paired readout (b00036-b00046). The different collection/holding interval, post-RT pooling, and sample-dependent downstream handling are located at the correct material stages. The right nucleus-isolation operation is outside the selected droplet-to-readout group and remains explicitly unmatched. Group inspection confirms both groups' complete biological and bead inputs and sequence-data outputs, with no hidden alternative route. These necessary retained connections and coordinated revisions are covered without splitting broad operations or inventing internal IDs. The authors' concentration explanation and jointly optimized loading result are distinguished from an isolated recovery/sensitivity benefit. The assertion is neither duplicated as component_adoption nor expanded to every preparation, analysis, protocol version, or a unique parent.

    "left_evidence": [
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "Drop-Seq consists of the following steps (Figure 1A): (1) prepare a single-cell suspension from a tissue; (2) co-encapsulate each cell with a distinctly barcoded microparticle (bead) in a nanoliter-scale droplet; (3) lyse cells after they have been isolated in droplets; (4) capture a cell’s mRNAs on its companion microparticle, forming STAMPs (Single-cell Transcriptomes Attached to Microparticles); (5) reverse-transcribe, amplify, and sequence thousands of STAMPs in one reaction; and (6) use the STAMP barcodes to infer each transcript’s cell of origin."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "To deliver large numbers of distinctly barcoded primer molecules into individual droplets, we use microparticles (beads). We synthesized oligonucleotide primers directly on beads (from 5’ to 3’, yielding free 3’ ends available for enzymatic priming). Each oligonucleotide is composed of four parts (Figure 1B): (1) a constant sequence (identical on all primers and beads) for use as a priming site for downstream PCR and sequencing; (2) a “cell barcode” (identical across all the primers on the surface of any one bead, but different from the cell barcodes on other beads); (3) a Unique Molecular Identifier (UMI) (different on each primer, to identify PCR duplicates) (Kivioja et al., 2012); and (4) an oligo-dT sequence for capturing polyadenylated mRNAs and priming reverse transcription."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "We designed a microfluidic “co-flow” device (Utada et al., 2007) to co-encapsulate cells with barcoded microparticles (Figures 2A, S2 and DataFile 1). This device quickly co-flows two aqueous solutions across an oil channel to form more than 100,000 nanoliter-sized droplets per minute. One flow contains the barcoded microparticles suspended in a lysis buffer; the other flow contains a cell suspension (Figure 2A, left, Figure 2B). The number of droplets created greatly exceeds the number of beads or cells injected, so that a droplet will generally contain zero or one cells, and zero or one beads. Millions of nanoliter-sized droplets are generated per hour, of which thousands contain both a bead and a cell (Movie S1). STAMPs are produced in the subset of droplets that contain both a bead and a cell."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "To efficiently process thousands of STAMPs at once, we break droplets, collect the mRNA-bound microparticles, and reverse-transcribe the mRNAs (from the microparticle-attached primers) together in one reaction, forming covalent, stable STAMPs (Figure 2A, step 7, and Experimental Procedures). A scientist can then select any desired number of STAMPs for the preparation of 3’-end digital expression libraries (Figure 2C, Experimental Procedures). We sequence the resulting molecules from each end (Figure 2C) using high-capacity parallel sequencing. We digitally count the number of mRNA transcripts of each gene ascertained in each cell, using the UMIs to avoid double-counting sequence reads that arose from the same mRNA transcript. We thereby create a matrix of digital gene-expression measurements (one measurement per gene per cell) for further analysis (Figure 2D, Experimental Procedures)."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "Monodisperse droplets ~1 nL in size were generated using the microfluidic device described in Extended Experimental Procedures, in which barcoded microparticles, suspended in lysis buffer, were flowed at a rate equal to that of a single-cell suspension, so that resulting droplets were composed of an equal amount of each component. As soon as droplet generation was complete, droplets were broken with perfluorooctanol in 30 mL of 6x SSC. The addition of a large aqueous volume to the droplets reduces hybridization events after droplet breakage, because DNA base pairing follows second-order kinetics (Britten and Kohne, 1968; Wetmur and Davidson, 1968). The beads were then washed and resuspended in a reverse transcriptase mix, followed by a treatment with exonuclease I to remove unextended primers. The beads were then washed, counted, aliquoted into PCR tubes, and PCR amplified. The PCR reactions were purified and pooled, and the amplified cDNA quantified on a BioAnalyzer High Sensitivity Chip (Agilent). The cDNA was fragmented and amplified for sequencing with the Nextera XT DNA sample prep kit (Illumina) using custom primers that enabled the specific amplification of only the 3’ ends (Table S6). The libraries were purified, quantified, and then sequenced on the Illumina NextSeq 500. All details regarding reaction conditions, primers used, and sequencing specifications can be found in the Extended Experimental Procedures."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "One potential mode of failure in any single-cell method involves cells that stick together or happen to otherwise be co-isolated for library preparation. In Drop-Seq, across four conditions spanning 12.5 cells/µL to 100 cells/µL, the fraction of species-mixed STAMPs correlated with cell concentration (Figure 3A, 3B, S3B; Experimental Procedures), with cell doublet estimates ranging from 0.36% to 11.3% for the various cell concentrations tested (under the assumption that human-mouse doublets account for half of all doublets). This reflects the greater chance at higher cell concentrations that a droplet could encapsulate multiple cells. By comparison, previous studies that used FACS (Jaitin et al., 2014) or a commercial microfluidics platform (Shalek et al., 2014) to isolate single cells reported doublet rates of 2.3% and 11% respectively, based upon examining microscopy images of captured cells. In analyzing the above mouse-human cell suspension mixture in a commercial microfluidics system (Fluidigm C1), we found that 30% of the resulting libraries in that experiment were species-mixed (Figure S3C); about one-third of these doublets were visible in the microscopy images."
      }

    "right_evidence": [
      {
        "source_id": "right:primary:paper:28846088",
        "quote": "Here, we addressed this challenge by developing DroNc-seq (Fig. 1a), a massively parallel single nucleus RNA-seq method that combines the advantages of sNuc-Seq and Drop-seq to profile nuclei at low cost and high throughput. We modified Drop-seq7 to accommodate the relatively lower amount of RNA in nuclei compared to cells, including a modified microfluidic design and changes in the nuclei isolation protocol (Supplementary Fig. 1, Supplementary Table 1, Supplementary Data 1, Methods)."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "right:primary:paper:28846088",
        "quote": "Nuclei were isolated with EZ PREP buffer (Sigma, Cat #NUC-101). Tissue samples cut into pieces < 0.5 cm or cell pellets were homogenized using a glass dounce tissue grinder (Sigma, Cat #D8938) (25 times with pastel A, and 25 times with pastel B) in 2 ml of ice-cold EZ PREP and incubated on ice for 5 minutes, with additional 2 ml ice-cold EZ PREP. Nuclei were centrifuged at 500 x g for 5 minutes at 4°C, washed with 4 ml ice-cold EZ PREP and incubated on ice for 5 minutes. After centrifugation, the nuclei were washed in 4 ml Nuclei Suspension Buffer (NSB; consisting of 1x PBS, 0.01% BSA and 0.1% RNAse inhibitor (Clontech, Cat #2313A)). Isolated nuclei were resuspended in 2 ml NSB, filtered through a 35 μm cell strainer (Corning, Cat # 352235) and counted. A final concentration of 300,000 nuclei/ml was used for DroNc-seq experiments."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "right:primary:paper:28846088",

      },
      {
        "source_id": "right:primary:paper:28846088",
        "quote": "Barcoded beads (Chemgenes, Cat # Macosko-2011-10) were prepared as in Ref 7. Because the DroNc-seq microfluidic device has narrow channels (~70 μm), they are likely to clog from large beads, compared to Drop-seq. We therefore size-selected beads < 40 μm diameter using a strainer (PluriSelect, Cat # 43-50040-03); in our experience, these smaller beads comprise roughly 55% of the purchased bead pool. The barcoded beads were suspended in DLB (described above) and counted at 1:1 dilution in 20% PEG solution using a hemocytometer (VWR, Cat # 22-600-102)7, at concentrations between 325,000 and 350,000 per ml."
      },

        "source_id": "right:primary:paper:28846088",
        "quote": "The nuclei and barcoded bead suspension were loaded7 and flown at 1.5 ml/hr each, along with carrier oil (BioRad Sciences, Cat # 186-4006) at 16 ml/hr, to co-encapsulate single nuclei and beads in ~75 μm drops (vol. ~ 200 pl) at 4,500 drops/sec and double Poisson loading concentrations. The smaller droplet volume in DroNc-seq results in higher mRNA concentration in drops (> 5x) compared to 125 μm drops in Drop-seq."

        "source_id": "right:primary:paper:28846088",
        "quote": "The theoretical Poisson loading concentration at 1/10 bead and nuclei occupancy for devices with channels 70 μm wide and 75 μm deep is ~520,000/ml, and 100 μm depth (also tested) is 340,000/ml. We tested bead and cell loading at this and other concentrations using species-mixing experiments7 (e.g., Supplementary Fig. 1f and Supplementary Table 1) and ease of bead flow as metrics and found that beads at 350,000/ml and nuclei at 300,000/ml concentrations performed best, in terms of low human-mouse doublet rate and fewer clogging events during droplet generation. At the nuclei loading concentrations used, the occurrence of one or more nuclei in a drop follows a Poisson distribution, P(x) = λx e−λ/x!, where λ = Poisson parameter, and x=2 for doublet estimation. As a theoretical lower bound, increasing nuclei concentration will increase doublet rate as λ2 e−λ/2; e.g., if nuclei loading is increased by 10%, the probability of getting two nuclei in a drop will increase from 0.21% to 0.25%. However, the probability of getting two or more nuclei in a drop, i.e., doublets, triplets, etc., all of which would be indistinguishable in species-mixing experiments, is P(x ≥ 2; λ = 0.07) = 0.5%. In practice, nuclei that stick together or cellular debris could also contribute to doublets or doublet-like phenomena. Empirical doublet rates in experiments ranged from ~1% (mouse tissue; clustering analysis) to ~5% (species-mixing)."

    "evidence": [
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "To deliver large numbers of distinctly barcoded primer molecules into individual droplets, we use microparticles (beads). We synthesized oligonucleotide primers directly on beads (from 5’ to 3’, yielding free 3’ ends available for enzymatic priming). Each oligonucleotide is composed of four parts (Figure 1B): (1) a constant sequence (identical on all primers and beads) for use as a priming site for downstream PCR and sequencing; (2) a “cell barcode” (identical across all the primers on the surface of any one bead, but different from the cell barcodes on other beads); (3) a Unique Molecular Identifier (UMI) (different on each primer, to identify PCR duplicates) (Kivioja et al., 2012); and (4) an oligo-dT sequence for capturing polyadenylated mRNAs and priming reverse transcription."
      },
      {
        "source_id": "left:drop-seq::paper:26000488",
        "quote": "Monodisperse droplets ~1 nL in size were generated using the microfluidic device described in Extended Experimental Procedures, in which barcoded microparticles, suspended in lysis buffer, were flowed at a rate equal to that of a single-cell suspension, so that resulting droplets were composed of an equal amount of each component. As soon as droplet generation was complete, droplets were broken with perfluorooctanol in 30 mL of 6x SSC. The addition of a large aqueous volume to the droplets reduces hybridization events after droplet breakage, because DNA base pairing follows second-order kinetics (Britten and Kohne, 1968; Wetmur and Davidson, 1968). The beads were then washed and resuspended in a reverse transcriptase mix, followed by a treatment with exonuclease I to remove unextended primers. The beads were then washed, counted, aliquoted into PCR tubes, and PCR amplified. The PCR reactions were purified and pooled, and the amplified cDNA quantified on a BioAnalyzer High Sensitivity Chip (Agilent). The cDNA was fragmented and amplified for sequencing with the Nextera XT DNA sample prep kit (Illumina) using custom primers that enabled the specific amplification of only the 3’ ends (Table S6). The libraries were purified, quantified, and then sequenced on the Illumina NextSeq 500. All details regarding reaction conditions, primers used, and sequencing specifications can be found in the Extended Experimental Procedures."

Smart-seq → Nuc-Seq · component_adoption

Retained / shared arrangement

Scoped historical contribution: template-switch-mediated end tagging of an RNA-derived cDNA to support full-length cDNA synthesis and subsequent amplification. Ramskold's original Smart-seq demonstrates terminal nucleotide addition followed by SMARTer II A-templated extension and an anchor for second-strand synthesis. Krishnaswami uses this kind of template switching within its explicitly adopted Smart-seq2 cDNA procedure and cites Ramskold as reference 4 for the mechanism/full-length-cDNA rationale. Only that cited contribution is assigned a transfer, not every transformation in the selected operations.

What changed

Retained component arrangement: a growing RNA-derived cDNA acquires an oligo-templated terminal anchor, which supports further cDNA synthesis and then amplification. 1. Earlier versus destination implementation: left uses the SMARTer II A oligo in the original Smart-seq total-RNA/whole-cell-lysate procedure (b00031). Right places template switching inside nuclear lysis/cDNA synthesis from sorted nuclei and follows the Smart-seq2 procedure attributed to Picelli, reference 3 (b00008, b00251). The adoption destination is the copying/end-tagging part of synthesize_cdna, not tissue freezing, nuclei isolation/FACS or the whole Nuc-Seq assay. 2. Specified adaptation/context: right reports an LNA-modified TSO with rGrG+G at its 3′ end and a 5′-biotinylated synthesis-oligo set (b00107, b00252); left reports SMARTer II A without a comparable disclosed terminal chemistry and reports its CDS primer without stated biotin. The sources therefore support retention of a mechanism with a specified different implementation, not identity of constructs or proof that each right chemical feature was newly introduced relative to the proprietary left oligo. The coordinated change to 21-cycle nuclear cDNA PCR is described in c2, outside this component's historical scope.

Reported consequence and limits

The later source describes fuller-length cDNA synthesis as the reason for using this template-switch route. Original Smart-seq independently demonstrates the procedure and improved coverage in its own experiments; Nuc-Seq's ten-nucleus cortical example demonstrates use in the destination context. These observations do not establish a numerical gain of the inherited component over original Smart-seq on matched input, nor that a full-length advantage arose from LNA alone. The reported 5′-biotin anti-concatamer rationale is a distinct later modification attributed to Zeisel (reference 11), with the Nuc-Seq authors' confirmation described as unpublished; it is not an invention or performance property assigned to Ramskold by this edge.

Reviewer reasoning

The component_adoption definition is met at the named template-switch end-tagging contribution and its destination, not at the complete assay level. The later Development of the protocol passage attributes its actual cDNA procedure to Smart-seq2, reference 3, and places reference 4 specifically on the full-length/second-strand template-switch mechanism. The registered citation identity and the original paper's reference list independently resolve reference 4 to Ramskold's supplied Smart-seq implementation (PMID 22820318), whereas reference 3 is Picelli's Smart-seq2 protocol (PMID 24385147). This is a mechanism attribution in the explanation of a procedure actually used, reinforced by b00251, rather than an inference from shared structure, a background citation, chronology or a performance comparison. At this component's scope the reconstruction is supported on both sides. Earlier b00031 starts with oligo(dT)-primed RNA-derived cDNA, describes terminal nucleotide addition, base-pairing of SMARTer II A, RT extension using that oligo as template, and the resulting universal anchor for second-strand synthesis. The retained contribution in right synthesize_cdna is template-switch acquisition of an oligo-derived terminal sequence on nuclear RNA-derived cDNA. Right b00008 establishes the mechanism and b00251 establishes deployment in the modified Smart-seq2 procedure after FACS; b00107 documents the destination TSO with rGrG+G/LNA and 5′ biotin and its oligo(dT) partner. These are destination specifications, not proof of the exact original commercial oligo chemistry or attribution of LNA/biotin to Ramskold. The product retains transcript information plus an amplification interface and next enters right amplify_cdna using the documented ISPCR oligo, producing DNA for library preparation (b00107, b00252, b00262). The 21-cycle modification belongs to the following amplification operation and is not counted as a second inherited contribution. The same complete group boundaries inspected for c1 locate this smaller historical contribution without adding internal IDs. The claim correctly does not meet or assert method_development from original Smart-seq: the later work explicitly identifies its own earlier nuclear assay, Grindberg reference 13, as its starting method, with a Tang cDNA procedure replaced by Smart-seq2 and gradient cleanup made optional (b00006/b00008). No independently established external development chain is asserted. Missing independent Picelli, Zeisel and Grindberg texts limits those distinct histories, not the directly attributed Ramskold mechanism claim, for which both earlier demonstration and later attribution/use are accessible. The Zeisel-attributed biotin modification, unpublished anti-concatamer confirmation, fuller-length rationale and whole-workflow observations are kept separate from inheritance of performance or tissue compatibility. No duplicate historical label is assigned to the same contribution.

Exact claim, evidence and provenance

iCLIP → iCLIP (Huppertz protocol) · method_development

Retained / shared arrangement

Scoped development of the iCLIP cDNA-conversion and library-recovery arrangement demonstrated by König et al. (PMID 20601959) into the standard UV-C Huppertz protocol (PMID 24184352). Retained arrangement: an RT primer supplies barcodes and two inverse adapter regions separated by BamHI; RT on antibody-selected, peptide-bearing RNA generates predominantly truncated cDNA; cDNA self-circularization supplies the second amplification interface without requiring passage through the peptide; complementary-oligo annealing, BamHI cleavage and adapter-primer PCR recover a linear DNA library. The selected groups begin with peptide-bearing RNA plus the RT primer and end with amplified DNA. Upstream crosslinking and purification provide context, not an assertion that every original component was inherited.

What changed

Retained arrangement is specified above. Distinct changes/qualifications within the selected library portion: 1. RT-primer information architecture (both reverse_transcribe operations): the left Methods specify a 3-nt random molecule tag plus a 2-nt experiment tag at the primer 5′ region; the right specifies 5 random bases (NN...NNN around the sample code) plus a 4-nt sample barcode, with sample codes separated by at least two substitutions. These are DNA sequences incorporated by RT, not labels on the original protein or a cell identity. Both retain barcode-associated cDNA endpoints for later duplicate filtering; the changed code lengths and sample-code spacing are meaningful distinctions that need no new operation. 2. RNA removal before cDNA circularization (right remove_rna_select; left circularize boundary): the left main Methods describe denaturing cDNA size purification before circularization but do not specify alkaline RNA removal. The right explicitly applies NaOH after RT and before gel isolation to remove residual, potentially radioactive template RNA. Table 1 calls this an addition relative to the 2011 JoVE protocol [19], not a demonstrated absence in the 2010 implementation. The right group therefore receives cDNA plus residual RNA, while the left boundary abstracts the recovered DNA product. 3. Post-gel cleanup (right remove_rna_select leading to all three circularize operations; left circularize): the left main text reports size-purified cDNA followed by single-stranded DNA ligase without resolving this cleanup. The right recovers each gel band and performs phenol/chloroform extraction before CircLigase II. Table 1 describes adding this extraction relative to JoVE to remove urea/polyacrylamide that could inhibit circularization. This is a documented later preparation with an incompletely specified original counterpart, not evidence that 2010 lacked extraction. 4. Fraction handling (left circularize/linearize_pcr versus the complete connected right remove_rna_select–three circularize/linearize_pcr branches–pool_libraries group): the left paper says cDNA was size-purified but the existing graph contains one recovered-cDNA route. The right isolates 70–80, 80–100 and 100–150 nt total-cDNA bands, processes them separately through circularization, BamHI and PCR, then recommends low:middle:high pooling at 1:5:5, subject to library quality and RNA interests. The 52-nt adapter/primer contribution means band length is not insert length. The two RNA fractions that the left paper recovered from nitrocellulose BEFORE RT are not these three cDNA fractions AFTER RT. The right branching is explicitly represented; no three original branches or original pooling operation can be invented. 5. Preparative amplification (left linearize_pcr versus all three right linearize_pcr operations): the left main Methods specify adapter-complementary PCR without resolving optimization versus preparative reaction volume. The right selects cycle numbers by a pilot PCR and uses a larger preparative volume to reduce salt inhibition from concentrated cDNA. Table 1 contrasts increased preparative volume with the same-volume JoVE procedure; the corresponding 2010 volume is not established. The volume/concentration change is one combined intervention, not separately proven gains from dilution and volume.

Reported consequence and limits

No head-to-head estimate of recovery, specificity, nucleotide accuracy, barcode collision rate or overall performance of these two versions is established. The left demonstrates molecular duplicate discrimination and transcriptome-wide hnRNP C crosslink-associated cDNA counts. On the right, alkaline treatment was tested by radioactive counts/s and recovery of PCR product with two RT primers; Fig. 4B shows loss of remaining radioactive signal with similar PCR product, rather than an increase in recovered cDNA. The proposed avoidance of circularization inhibition is author rationale. The post-gel extraction and larger preparative-PCR volume benefits are reported in Table 1 relative to JoVE, without a quantified 2010 comparator. PCR-cycle testing is specific to an hnRNP C library (21–39 cycles; 27–29 recommended in that experiment), not a universal cycle count or evidence of improved performance over the left. The longer barcodes have stated functions; their causal benefit over the original 3/2-nt design is not tested here.

Reviewer reasoning

The shared method_development definition is met at the stated cDNA-conversion/library-recovery scope: a documented starting assay with a retained and revised connected arrangement. Huppertz Introduction [b00007] identifies the authors' previously developed iCLIP as reference [8]; [b00124] and the registered citation context identify that implementation as König 2010, PMID 20601959, DOI 10.1038/nsmb.1838. The abstract explicitly presents the current assay as an improved iCLIP protocol, and the current Methods independently implement the retained arrangement. This combination establishes the assay starting point and revision; the decision does not rest on a background citation, shared structure or publication dates. The Table 1 reference [19] is instead JoVE 2011, PMID 21559008, DOI 10.3791/2638. The claim correctly does not turn this into a direct, fully reconstructed 2010-to-2013 version edit, nor count the same contribution again as component_adoption. Earlier-to-later reconstruction: König [b00033] and Figure 1a [b00088] establish that antibody-selected RNA carrying a ligated 3′ adapter and residual covalent peptide is copied with a DNA primer containing two inverse adapter regions separated by BamHI, a 2-nt experiment code and a 3-nt random molecule code. Peptide-associated stopping produces cDNA whose termination boundary and primer barcode remain associated. Denaturing size purification precedes single-stranded DNA ligation into circles; a restriction-site-complementary oligonucleotide enables BamHI opening between the adapter regions, and adapter-complementary PCR produces linear amplified DNA for subsequent sequencing. Thus the original paper demonstrates the connected arrangement, not merely the availability of a barcode or ligase. Huppertz [b00008], [b00020], [b00077–86], [b00094] and Figure 1 retain peptide-dependent RT endpoints, primer-derived adapter/barcode DNA, circularization, oligo-assisted BamHI opening and PCR. The later primer has 5 random and 4 sample bases, with sample codes differing by at least two positions. This is an evidenced information-design difference, with no measured comparative collision/error benefit asserted. In the later route, NaOH treatment after RT removes residual RNA before cDNA isolation. Three total-cDNA bands (70–80, 80–100 and 100–150 nt, including 52 nt of primer/adapter sequence) are separately recovered, cleaned by phenol/chloroform extraction, circularized with CircLigase II, annealed to Cut_oligo, cut with BamHI and amplified before pooling. The default 1:5:5 pool is adjustable. Pilot PCR guides cycle choice; the larger preparative reaction and concentrated template are discussed as a coordinated concentration/volume intervention. These connections are supplied by the actual Methods and pooling instruction, not inferred from a common purpose. The material result is amplified linear DNA retaining the RNA-derived sequence and barcode-associated endpoint; subsequent sequencing/mapping can count recovered crosslink-associated cDNAs. It is not a chemical RNA–protein contact, a cell identifier or a complete census of binding. The group tool confirms that the exact three left IDs form one connected group with peptide_rna and rt_primer inputs and cdna_library output. The exact nine right IDs have the corresponding two conceptual inputs and one pooled library output; all three cDNA→circle→PCR paths are internal, with no omitted outgoing size branch. The upstream proteinase operation is outside each selected group, as claimed. The left graph does not expose the intermediate cleanup or fraction routes, and the claim leaves those as paper annotations rather than inventing IDs. Change coverage is adequate at this scope. The record includes the barcode change and the later RNA-removal, post-gel cleanup, fraction processing/pooling and preparative-PCR procedures, while explicitly leaving the corresponding unreported 2010 details unknown. Table 1's changes and benefits are correctly anchored to JoVE, not attributed as observed 2010 before-values. The alkaline-treatment test [b00081]/Fig. 4B measures residual radioactivity and PCR product with two primers: radioactive signal disappears while PCR product is similar. It does not demonstrate more recovered cDNA. Proposed relief of circularization inhibition, cleanup benefits and preparative-PCR benefits are kept separate from this observation and from a direct two-version performance comparison. The missing JoVE primary and complete original Supplementary Methods limit the version chain and fine comparisons, but do not remove the independently evidenced starting arrangement, retained route or barcode revision actually claimed.

Drop-seq → Seq-Well · component_adoption

Retained / shared arrangement

Adopted contribution: the Drop-seq barcoded poly(dT) mRNA-capture bead design used to bind cellular transcripts to oligos bearing a bead-specific cell barcode, primer-level UMI and universal priming sequence. It functions inside the existing lyse_capture operations; the assertion is limited to the bead/capture-primer contribution, not inheritance of either combined lysis/capture operation as a whole.

What changed

Retained contribution: the local capture bead carries the cellular and molecular identifiers before reverse transcription. 1. Context adaptation: left uses these beads as companions to cells in droplets; right explicitly reports using the same mRNA capture beads (Drop-Seq, reference 12), but deploys them in membrane-sealed PDMS wells. The changed compartment and lysis delivery are C1–C2, not a new oligo design. 2. Information interface retained: left describes free 3′ primer ends, a cell barcode shared on a bead and UMIs varying among primers; right describes the same functional segments and split-and-pool barcode construction in its Supplementary Table 1 caption. Captured mRNA reaches downstream preparation through hybridization to the tagged bead oligo, with the tags incorporated into cDNA later. The available right text reports no specific primer redesign that should be invented as a difference.

Reported consequence and limits

The right paper’s species-mixing libraries demonstrate actual use of the bead contribution in the new well/membrane context. The earlier paper independently demonstrates recognition of bead-origin barcodes using synthetic-RNA cDNAs and cellular libraries. No isolated performance gain from adopting the beads is established. The earlier bead experiment’s barcode separation and the later assay’s transcript/gene yields are not transferable reagent guarantees.

Reviewer reasoning

Accept as written under the component_adoption definition: a named adopted contribution and its later destination are both established. The contribution is the barcoded poly(dT) capture-bead/oligo design, not either whole lysis/capture operation. Macosko b00011-13 independently describes attached primers with free 3-prime ends, a universal site, bead-shared cell barcode, primer-varying UMI and oligo(dT), and demonstrates recovery of distinct bead-origin barcodes after reverse transcription and sequencing of synthetic RNAs. Its droplet implementation captures cellular RNA on these primers before bead recovery and bulk copying. Gierahn b00012 explicitly identifies actual use of the same mRNA capture beads as Drop-Seq with citation 12; b00011 and the Supplementary Table 1 caption describe their implemented capture and information functions. Reference 12 is independently verified as Macosko, PMID 26000488, DOI 10.1016/j.cell.2015.05.002. In Seq-Well the retained bead design instead captures RNA in membrane-sealed PDMS wells; the resulting RNA/primer association supplies the subsequent recovered-bead bulk preparation. The later species-mixing experiment demonstrates use in that destination. No primer redesign or independent gain is needed for unchanged adoption, and none is invented. Group boundaries support locating this smaller contribution within both existing lyse_capture blocks without inheriting their distinct lysis procedures. This reagent-design assertion is distinct from C5's procedural adoption.

Drop-seq → Seq-Well · component_adoption

Retained / shared arrangement

Adopted contribution: the bulk reverse-transcription, amplification and library-preparation procedure applied to recovered mRNA-capture beads, attributed by Seq-Well to Macosko’s Drop-seq implementation. This is the preparation portion of right prepare_library and left make_library. It excludes the different compartment-opening/recovery mechanisms, and excludes sequencing from the scoped graph correspondence.

What changed

Retained procedural arrangement: bead-bound captured RNAs are copied and amplified in bulk while retaining bead-primer identifiers, rather than reverse transcribing each cell in a separate reaction vessel. 1. Entry context: left make_library receives pooled beads after droplet disruption; right prepare_library receives captured RNA in sealed wells and includes membrane peeling and bead removal before the adopted bulk procedure. That right recovery component is outside this adoption assertion. 2. Destination: both preparations yield barcoded cDNA-derived libraries, but right embeds the procedure after its membrane-based capture system; no altered RT enzyme, buffer or amplification gain is documented in the available attribution passage. Seq-Well also attributes paired-end sequencing in the same sentence, but its graph stops at the physical library, so that part of the cited use remains paper context rather than an invented operation.

Reported consequence and limits

Actual use in Seq-Well demonstrates that its recovered capture beads can feed this bulk procedure in the reported species-mixing, PBMC and macrophage settings. No gain is assigned to adopting the bulk procedure itself, and the attribution does not establish compatibility with arbitrary alternative bead chemistries, RNAs or library designs. The later whole-assay results cannot isolate the effect of this retained procedure.

Reviewer reasoning

Accept as written under the component_adoption definition: the identifiable adopted contribution is the recovered-bead bulk reverse-transcription/amplification/library procedure, and its destination is the preparation portion of Seq-Well's existing prepare_library block. The decisive Gierahn b00011 sentence attaches 'as previously described12' to the named bulk processing and paired-end sequencing, and reference 12 identifies the actual Macosko Drop-seq implementation. This is procedural attribution and actual use, not an inference from similar structure or chronology. Independently, Macosko b00017/b00056 establishes the earlier arrangement: captured polyadenylated RNA remains hybridized to attached tagged primers on beads recovered from droplets; bulk reverse transcription creates stable covalent cDNA products, followed by ExoI treatment, PCR amplification and cDNA library preparation. In the later destination, membrane peeling and bead removal supply captured-RNA beads to the retained bulk copying/amplification/library arrangement. Barcode/UMI-bearing primers carry bead and molecule attribution into the resulting DNA library, enabling the subsequently reported paired-end measurement. Right recovery is inside the selected broad block but expressly outside the adopted contribution; sequencing is paper context beyond the graph endpoint. Attribution supports the named procedure without proving identity of every enzyme, condition or internal implementation. This satisfies component adoption rather than a documented starting assay with a revised whole-assay arrangement. C3 concerns the capture-bead design; C5 concerns the downstream procedure, so they do not double-count one historical assertion or apply two history labels to it.
